=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a directed graph with 2N vertices and 2N-1 edges.
The vertices are numbered 1, 2, \ldots, 2N, and the i-th edge is a directed edge from vertex i to vertex i+1.
You are given a length-2N string S = S_1 S_2 \ldots S_{2N} consisting of N Ws and N Bs.
Vertex i is colored white if S_i is W, and black if S_i is B.
You will perform the following series of operations:

- Partition the 2N vertices into N pairs, each consisting of one white vertex and one black vertex.
- For each pair, add a directed edge from the white vertex to the black vertex.

Print the number, modulo 998244353, of ways to partition the vertices into N pairs such that the final graph is strongly connected.
 Notes on strongly connectedness
A directed graph is strongly connected if and only if it is possible to travel from any vertex to any vertex by following edges.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
S

Output

Print the number, modulo 998244353, of ways to partition the vertices into N pairs so that the final graph is strongly connected.

Constraints


- 1 \le N \le 2\times 10^5
- S is a length 2N string consisting of N Ws and N Bs.
- N is an integer.

Sample Input 1

2
BWBW

Sample Output 1

1

Vertices 2,4 are white, and vertices 1,3 are black.
Let (u,v) denote an edge from vertex u to vertex v.
If we pair up vertices as (2,1), (4,3), the final graph have the edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (2,1), (4,3). In this case, for example, it is impossible to travel from vertex 3 to vertex 1 by following edges, so this graph is not strongly connected.
If we pair up vertices as (2,3), (4,1), the final graph have the edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (2,3), (4,1). This graph is strongly connected.
Therefore, there is exactly 1 way to pair up the vertices that satisfies the condition.

Sample Input 2

4
BWWBWBWB

Sample Output 2

0

No matter how you pair up the vertices, you cannot satisfy the condition.

Sample Input 3

9
BWWBWBBBWWBWBBWWBW

Sample Output 3

240792


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Alternatively, the sequential placement process with state e can be optimized because transitions only depend on e and d_v. We can maintain a DP array over e after each step. Let's explore.

At step v (from N down to 1), before placing v, state e = e_{v+1} (min position of values >v). It ranges 1..v+1 (since placed N-v values, min <= v+1). We need count ways to have each e. Then place value v:
- If e <= d_v:
   * choose p < e: e' = p for p=1..e-1, 1 way each.
   * choose p > e: e' = e, with v-e+1 ways.
- If e > d_v:
   * choose p <= d_v: e' = p for p=1..d_v, 1 way each. (Since e>d_v, p<e)
This transition from e to e' <= e. It is like:
For each state e, if e <= d_v, it contributes:
   to same e: (v - e + 1) * dp[e]
   to all smaller e' < e: dp[e] (one for each e')
If e > d_v:
   to all e' <= d_v: dp[e] (one for each e')
No contribution to e' > e.

We need after processing v=1, sum dp[e]? At v=1, after placing value1, e_1=1 always; all positions filled. The final count is dp[1] after step 1. We can process all v. N up to 2e5, naive O(N^2) due to range additions. Need optimize with data structures.

But with constraints d_v, maybe we can maintain dp in compressed form. Let's examine transition structure more.

Let A[x] = dp_{v+1}[x] for x=1..v+1. Define B[x] for x=1..L (L=min(d_v,v)):
B[x] = S[x+1] + (v+1 - x) A[x], where S[x+1]=∑_{e=x+1}^{v+1} A[e].

This is linear. We can express B in terms of prefix sums of A. Let P[x]=∑_{e=1}^x A[e]. Total T=P[v+1]. S[x+1]=T-P[x].
So B[x] = T - P[x] + (v+1 - x) A[x].

If we maintain A and prefix sums, computing B for all x is O(L). Need avoid O(N^2) for unconstrained steps where L=v. But maybe we don't need all x; we only need eventually dp_1[1]. However constraints at future steps may require suffix sums of B at certain x. We need represent B compactly.

Observe B[x] is a combination of prefix sums and A[x]. This is like applying an operator that reduces length by 1. Repeated unconstrained application from A[x]=1 yields polynomial in x? Let's compute dp after k unconstrained steps maybe has form (N - x + something) * something? Let's derive.

Maybe there is a known formula: dp_v[x] = number of permutations of values v..N with minimum position x and satisfying constraints for thresholds >v. This can be expressed as:
dp_v[x] = (N-v)! * ? maybe times probability? Let's derive combinatorially.

Without constraints, after placing values v..N (k=N-v+1 values), the number of ways to place them such that minimum position is x is:
Choose positions for k values among N, with minimum x. The number of subsets of size k with min x is C(N-x, k-1). Then assign the k values to these positions: k! ways. But wait positions are assigned to specific values, and we process descending. For any set of k positions with min x, the number of bijections from values v..N to these positions is k!. So total = k! * C(N-x, k-1). Let's test N=3, after v=3 k=1: 1!*C(3-x,0)=1 for x=1..3. Good. After v=2 k=2: 2!*C(3-x,1)=2*(3-x): x=1->4,x=2->2. Matches. After v=1 k=3: 6*C(3-x,2), x=1 ->6*C(2,2)=6. Good.

At each step i (from 0 to N-1), we have f over m. Need compute next f for m=1..N. Transition:

- For all m<T, range add to next[T..N] of f[m].

This is like applying a linear operator. We can compute next array using suffix sums of f and weighted suffix sums:

next[x] =

     - from m > x? no, range add to m+1..N, so m < x.
So for x:

For a single q, number with M_q ≤ p is P(p,q) = p!/(p-q)! (choose ordered q values from [1..p], rest arbitrary (N-q)!) = (N-q)! * p!/(p-q)!.
For multiple constraints, because M_q nondecreasing, constraints M_{q_i} ≤ p_{q_i} with q_i increasing and p_i increasing. This is equivalent to the permutation's first q_i values all ≤p_i. Actually if first q_i values all ≤p_i, then the rectangle [1..q_i] × [p_i+1..N] contains no points. The forbidden board is a Young diagram (nested rectangles). The number of permutations avoiding a Ferrers board is given by the rook polynomial, which for Ferrers boards has a product formula? Yes! Permutations with forbidden positions forming a Ferrers board can be counted by the rook polynomial; the number of ways to place N nonattacking rooks avoiding a Ferrers board has a formula using inclusion-exclusion and the rook numbers of the board. For a Ferrers board with column heights, the number of permutations avoiding it is ∑ (-1)^k r_k (N-k)!, where r_k are rook numbers. Rook numbers of Ferrers boards have a product formula (Goldman-Joichi-White):
\[
\sum_{k=0}^N r_k (x)_{N-k} = \prod_{i=1}^N (x + b_i - i + 1)
\]
where b_i are column heights. This might be exactly what we need!

Let's derive DP for inclusion-exclusion.

We have constraints q=1..N-1 with thresholds p_q (nondecreasing, 0≤p_q≤N-1). Bad B_q: M_q ≤ p_q. Note if p_q < q, B_q impossible because first q distinct values max at least q > p_q. N(Q)=0 if any selected q has p_q < q? Actually if p_q < q, no permutation can have first q values all ≤p_q, so B_q empty. In inclusion-exclusion, such events can be ignored (they contribute 0). So only active q with p_q ≥ q matter. Let's define active constraints sorted q_1<q_2<...<q_K where p_{q_i} ≥ q_i. Since p_q nondecreasing, once p_q≥q? Not necessarily monotone of p_q-q; but active set maybe intervals? p_q nondecreasing, q increases, p_q-q can go up/down. Example p=[0,2] N=3: q1 p0<1 inactive, q2 p2=2 active. Active not prefix. But p_q nondecreasing.

For a subset Q of active constraints, N(Q) product formula:
Let selected q's be s_1 < s_2 < ... < s_m. For columns j:
- For j ≤ s_1, the smallest selected q ≥ j is s_1, so u_j = p_{s_1}.
- For s_{r-1} < j ≤ s_r, u_j = p_{s_r}.
- For j > s_m, u_j = N (no constraint).
Then N(Q) = ∏_{j=1}^N (u_j - j +1). Since u_j nondecreasing step function.

This product can be computed for a given Q by grouping intervals:
For interval j = L..R where u_j = u:
product over j=L..R of (u - j +1) = (u - L +1)_{R-L+1} falling factorial? Specifically ∏_{j=L}^R (u - j +1) = (u-L+1)! / (u-R)! if u≥R.
Let's define for selected s_r, interval (s_{r-1}+1 .. s_r) with u=p_{s_r}, except first interval 1..s_1 with u=p_{s_1}. Last interval s_m+1..N with u=N, product = ∏_{j=s_m+1}^N (N-j+1) = (N-s_m)!.

Thus N(Q) = [∏_{r=1}^m (p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})! / (p_{s_r} - s_r)!?] * (N-s_m)!? Need handle s_0=0. For interval L=s_{r-1}+1, R=s_r, u=p_{s_r}. Product = ∏_{j=L}^R (u-j+1) = (u-L+1)!/(u-R)! = (p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})! / (p_{s_r} - s_r)!.
Because u-L+1 = p - (s_{r-1}+1)+1 = p - s_{r-1}; u-R = p - s_r.
For last interval L=s_m+1,R=N,u=N: product = (N - s_m)! / 0! = (N-s_m)!.
So
N(Q) = (N - s_m)! * ∏_{r=1}^m \frac{(p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})!}{(p_{s_r} - s_r)!}.
This is valid if p_{s_r} ≥ s_r (active) and p_{s_r} ≥ s_{r-1} (true since p nondecreasing and s_{r-1}<s_r, p_{s_r}≥p_{s_{r-1}}? Could be p_{s_r} < s_{r-1}? Since p_{s_r}≥s_r>s_{r-1}, so yes). Good.

Now inclusion-exclusion sum over all subsets Q (selected active constraints) of (-1)^m N(Q). This looks like a DP over selected constraints with a multiplicative factor depending on previous selected s_{r-1}. Let's define DP over q.

Let F(q) = sum over subsets of active constraints among 1..q? We need handle the product factors that couple consecutive selected constraints via (p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})! and also the final (N-s_m)!.

We can define a DP for the product up to the last selected constraint s_m:
For a selected sequence s_1<...<s_m, the product part (excluding final) is:
∏_{r=1}^m \frac{(p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})!}{(p_{s_r} - s_r)!}, with s_0=0.
Let's define weight for selecting s as current, with previous selected t (or 0):
transition weight = \frac{(p_s - t)!}{(p_s - s)!}.
Then after selecting m, the total including final is (N - s_m)! times product. We need sum over all sequences (subsets) of (-1)^m (N - s_m)! ∏ transition.

This can be computed by DP:
Let dp[s] = sum over valid subsets ending with selected constraint s of (-1)^m ∏_{r=1}^m \frac{(p_{s_r} - s_{r-1})!}{(p_{s_r} - s_r)!} (including sign).
Then answer valid = ∑_{s active} dp[s] * (N - s)! + 1? Wait the empty subset Q=∅ corresponds to no bad events selected, N(∅)=N! (all permutations) with sign +1. In inclusion-exclusion, valid = ∑_{Q} (-1)^{|Q|} N(Q). The empty subset contributes N!. Our DP over nonempty subsets should include empty separately. Also subsets with inactive constraints contribute 0, so ignore. Thus answer = N! + ∑_{nonempty Q active} (-1)^m N(Q).

We can include an artificial start s_0=0 with p_0? For first selected s, transition from t=0: (p_s - 0)!/(p_s - s)! = p_s!/(p_s-s)! = P(p_s, s). This is the number of ways to fill first s columns with values ≤p_s, matching N(Q) for Q={s}: (N-s)! * P(p_s,s). Good.

DP recurrence:
For each active s, dp[s] = - [ transition from start: P(p_s, s) + ∑_{t active, t<s} dp[t] * \frac{(p_s - t)!}{(p_s - s)!} ]? Sign: each selected adds -1. Let's define dp[s] as signed sum for subsets ending at s, including the sign (-1)^m and product factors excluding final (N-s)!.
Then:
dp[s] = - P(p_s, s) - ∑_{t<s} dp[t] * \frac{(p_s - t)!}{(p_s - s)!}.
Because start has sign + (empty before), selecting s flips sign. For previous subset ending at t with sign (-1)^m, adding s gives sign (-1)^{m+1} = - dp[t].
Then answer = N! + ∑_{s active} dp[s] * (N - s)!.

Let's test with N=3 p=[1,2]. Active: q1 p=1≥1, q2 p=2≥2.
Compute factorials.
P(p1,1)=1!/(0!)=1.
dp1 = -1.

transition from t=1: (p2 -1)!/(p2-2)! = (1)!/(0)! =1.
dp2 = - [2 + dp1*1] = -[2-1] = -1.
Answer = N! 6 + dp1*(N-1)! = -1*2! = -2 + dp2*(N-2)! = -1*1! = -1 => 3. Matches.

p=[0,0] no active answer6. Good.

- pos4=2: σ2=4. Need M1≥2 => σ1=2 or3. M2 ok. Remaining after pos2: if σ1=2, remaining {1,3} for pos3,4: 2; if σ1=3, remaining {1,2}:2. total4.
- pos4=3: σ3=4. Need M2≥3 and M1≥2. First two from {1,2,3} with max≥3 and first≥2. Possibilities (2,3),(3,1),(3,2) =3. Remaining pos4 one value: 3. Total pos4=3 gives3.
- pos4=4: M3=3<4 fail.
Total 6+4+3=13. Matches! Great.

Formula:

DP:

Let s be 1-index q, p = p_q. Recurrence:
dp[s] = - ( fact[p] * invfact[p-s] + invfact[p-s] * ∑_{t active, t<s} dp[t] * fact[p - t] )
= - invfact[p-s] * ( fact[p] + ∑_{t<s} dp[t] * fact[p - t] ).

Here t is previous selected constraint index (1..s-1). Note t can be any active constraint less than s. We need compute for each active s the sum ∑_{t<s} dp[t] * fact[p_s - t]. Since p_s is nondecreasing with s (p_q nondecreasing), and t<s. This is like a convolution with factorial depending on p_s - t. We can maintain a data structure as we scan s increasing.

Let s from 1 to N-1 (only active). We need query:
A(s) = ∑_{t < s, active} dp[t] * fact[p_s - t].
Then dp[s] = - invfact[p_s - s] * ( fact[p_s] + A(s) ).

After computing dp[s], we need add it to future queries: it contributes dp[s] * fact[p_{future} - s] for future p_{future} ≥ p_s. Since p_s nondecreasing, p_{future} - s varies.

We can maintain an array over t? Need support:

Let's derive from DP recurrence maybe it telescopes if we define a normalized variable.

Let dp[s] = - invfact[p_s - s] * B_s, where B_s = fact[p_s] + ∑_{t<s} dp[t] fact[p_s - t].

Let C_s = dp[s] fact[p_s - s]. Then:
C_s = - fact[p_s] - ∑_{t<s} dp[t] fact[p_s - t].
But dp[t] = C_t / fact[p_t - t]. So:
C_s = - fact[p_s] - ∑_{t<s} \frac{C_t}{fact[p_t - t]} fact[p_s - t].

Not obviously telescoping.

Maybe define E_s = C_s / fact[p_s]? Then:
E_s = -1 - ∑_{t<s} E_t * fact[p_t - t]? Let's compute:

E_s fact[p_s] = - fact[p_s] - ∑_{t<s} E_t fact[p_t] / fact[p_t-t] * fact[p_s - t].
Divide fact[p_s]:
E_s = -1 - ∑_{t<s} E_t * \frac{fact[p_t]}{fact[p_t-t]} * \frac{fact[p_s-t]}{fact[p_s]}.
The ratio fact[p_s-t]/fact[p_s] = 1 / [(p_s)(p_s-1)...(p_s-t+1)] = 1 / (p_s)_t (falling). Not telescoping.

Let's see if CDQ with NTT is acceptable in Python for N=2e5. Mod 998244353, NTT convolution. We need implement CDQ recursively:
dp array length M = N (0..N-1 for q=1..N-1). Only active positions have nonzero dp. We need compute dp[s] = -invfact[p[s]-s] * (fact[p[s]] + sum_{t<s} dp[t]*fact[p[s]-t]).
This is a recurrence where dp[s] depends on all previous dp[t] with kernel fact[p[s]-t]. Since p[s] is nondecreasing, but not necessarily equal to s. In CDQ, for a range [l,r), we recursively compute left, then use NTT to add contributions from left to right, then recurse right. The contribution from t in left to s in right is dp[t] * fact[p[s]-t]. We need compute for all s in right:
add[s] = ∑_{t∈left} dp[t] * fact[p[s]-t].
This is not a standard convolution with index s because p[s] not linear. However, we can compute it by convolution if we create an array A indexed by t (0..N) with A[t]=dp[t] for t in left, and B[k]=fact[k]. Then (A*B)[r] = ∑_t A[t] fact[r-t]. We need r = p[s]. So for each s, add[s] = conv[p[s]]. Great! Since p[s] is an integer index. So in each CDQ node, we can compute convolution of left dp with fact, and for each s in right, take conv[p[s]]. This works regardless of p[s] order, as long as p[s] is within range. The restriction t<s is satisfied because left t < s (CDQ split by index). Also need t active; A[t]=dp[t] for active in left, 0 otherwise.

Thus CDQ NTT is straightforward:
- fact array length N+1 (fact[0..N]).
- dp array length N (0..N-1 for q=1..N-1), initialized 0.
- For s from 0 to N-1:
   if p[s] >= s+1 (active):
      val = fact[p[s]] + sum_{t<s} dp[t]*fact[p[s]-t]
      dp[s] = - val * invfact[p[s] - (s+1)]? Wait careful with 0-index.

Let's convert indices carefully.

Let q = s+1 (1..N-1). p_q = p[s]. Active if p_q ≥ q = s+1.
Recurrence for q:
dp_q = - invfact[p_q - q] * ( fact[p_q] + ∑_{t=1}^{q-1} dp_t * fact[p_q - t] ).
Here t is previous constraint index (1..q-1), not necessarily active? dp_t=0 if inactive. In 0-index, let t_idx = t-1. Then:
dp0[s] = dp_q.
sum over t_idx < s of dp0[t_idx] * fact[p[s] - (t_idx+1)].
Because p_q - t = p[s] - (t_idx+1).
So kernel index = p[s] - t_idx - 1.
Thus conv[r] = ∑_{t_idx} dp0[t_idx] * fact[r - t_idx]? If we want r = p[s]-1? Let's set A[t_idx] = dp0[t_idx], B[k]=fact[k]. Then (A*B)[r] = ∑_{t_idx} A[t_idx] fact[r - t_idx]. We need fact[p[s] - t_idx - 1] = fact[(p[s]-1) - t_idx]. So r = p[s]-1. Thus add = conv[p[s]-1].
Then val = fact[p[s]] + conv[p[s]-1].
dp0[s] = - val * invfact[p[s] - (s+1)] (since q=s+1).
Let's test with N=3 p=[1,2] (0-index p[0]=1,p[1]=2).
s=0 q=1 active p=1. sum none. val=fact1=1. invfact[p-q]=invfact[0]=1. dp0[0]=-1. Good.
s=1 q=2 p=2. Need sum t=1: dp1? dp0[0]=-1 * fact[p2 -1] = fact[1]=1. conv[p-1]=conv[1] = A[0]*fact[1] + A[1]*fact[0]? A[0]=-1, A[1]=0 => -1. val=fact2 + (-1)=2-1=1. invfact[p-q]=invfact[0]=1. dp0[1]=-1. Good.

So CDQ computes conv[p[s]-1] for right s using left dp.

After dp computed, answer = N! + ∑_{q active} dp_q * (N - q)!.

Now, is CDQ with NTT necessary? Maybe we can compute the convolution recurrence online with a Fenwick tree if we use factorial kernel and p[s] nondecreasing? Let's see if there is a simpler O(N) using the "online convolution" with arbitrary kernel fact[p[s]-t] and p[s] nondecreasing. Since p[s] is nondecreasing, the query index r=p[s]-1 is nondecreasing. We need point queries of a convolution of dp with fact at increasing r, while inserting new dp at index s. This is exactly online convolution where both insertion index and query index increase. There is a known technique using divide-and-conquer (CDQ) or "online FFT". CDQ is standard.

Can we do O(N log N) with a single NTT by treating it as a linear recurrence? Maybe not.

Let's estimate CDQ NTT in Python for N=2e5. Number of NTT calls at each level: at each node, convolution of left size L with fact size N+1? That would be too heavy if we convolve with full fact at every node. We only need conv up to max r in right, which is ≤N-1. But fact length N+1. Standard CDQ for online convolution: at each node [l,r), mid, compute convolution of A[l:mid] with B[0:r-l]? Wait we need conv[r'] for r' = p[s]-1. The maximum r' needed in this node is max p[s]-1 for s in right, which can be up to N-1. The minimum r' maybe. The convolution of left dp (length mid-l) with fact (length N+1) would be O(N log N) per node, too much. But we can truncate B to needed range. For a query r' = p[s]-1, the terms t in left satisfy t < mid ≤ s, and fact[r'-t] nonzero if r'≥t. Since r'=p[s]-1 ≥ s? Active p[s]≥s+1 => r'≥s. So r'-t ≥ s-t >0. The maximum t in left < mid, r' up to N-1. We need B indices up to r' - min_t. If we convolve A (length L) with B (length maybe max_r - min_t +1), the output length is L + lenB -1. But for each node, lenB could be O(N), leading O(N log^2 N) if doing full convolution at each node? Actually standard CDQ for online convolution where B is fixed length N: at each node, we convolve left segment with B segment? Let's recall.

We have recurrence f[i] = g[i] + ∑_{j<i} f[j] h[i-j] (standard online convolution). CDQ: at node [l,r), after left computed, compute contribution from left to right: for i in [mid,r), add ∑_{j∈[l,mid)} f[j] h[i-j]. This is a convolution of f[l:mid] with h[0:r-l] (or h up to r-l). The length of h needed is r-l, not N. Because i-j ≤ r-l. So per node convolution length O(node length). Total O(N log^2 N) if NTT at each node? Actually at each level, sum of node sizes = N, but NTT cost per node of size s is s log s; sum over nodes at a level = N log N, times log N levels = N log^2 N. However many implementations of CDQ with NTT are O(N log^2 N). For N=2e5 in Python, might be too slow.

But our kernel is fact[p[s]-t], not fact[s-t]. The index difference is not i-j but p[i]-j. Since p[i] can be up to N, and p[i] not equal i. For a node, for i in right, j in left, the kernel index p[i]-j ranges from p[i] - (mid-1) to p[i] - l. This depends on i. If we convolve A[j] with B[k]=fact[k], output at index p[i] (or p[i]-1). The needed B length is max p[i]-l - min p[i]-(mid-1)? Actually for all i in right, kernel indices p[i]-j. - Divide indices t into blocks.
- For completed blocks, precompute their convolution with fact and add to an array C at block level? Let's search memory of AtCoder constraints: N=2e5, mod 998244353. They often expect O(N log N) with NTT or O(N). Python solutions may use NTT for convolution once. Maybe the answer can be computed by a single convolution if we process constraints in order of p? Let's see.

The recurrence dp[s] depends on previous t with t<s. If we process s increasing, the sum is a convolution of dp with fact evaluated at p[s]-1. This is online convolution with query index p[s]-1. Since p[s] is nondecreasing, we can use a technique called "online convolution with monotone queries" using a single divide-and-conquer but maybe O(N log N) if we use NTT at each level with total O(N log N) not O(N log^2 N)? Actually standard CDQ for online convolution where h length is node size gives O(N log^2 N) if NTT per node, but there is a variant using a global NTT per level? Let's analyze.

In standard CDQ, at each node we convolve left segment with h[0:r-l]. The total cost per level is sum over nodes O((r-l) log(r-l)) = O(N log N) because sum sizes = N and log(size) ≤ log N. There are log N levels, so O(N log^2 N). But if we use NTT for each node, yes. However, many implementations of CDQ for online convolution are O(N log^2 N). For N=2e5, in C++ fine; Python maybe not.

Can we reduce to O(N log N) by using a single NTT per level? At each level, the nodes are disjoint. We could batch all left segments and right queries? The kernel h is the same. Maybe we can avoid NTT by using the fact that fact[k] modulo MOD has an inverse and the recurrence can be solved with prefix sums if we multiply by something. Let's try to manipulate the recurrence with factorials more.

Recurrence:
dp_s = - \frac{1}{(p_s-s)!} \left( p_s! + \sum_{t<s} dp_t (p_s-t)! \right).

Let's define q_s = p_s - s. Then p_s = s + q_s.
dp_s = - \frac{1}{q_s!} \left( (s+q_s)! + \sum_{t<s} dp_t (s+q_s-t)! \right).

Let u = s-t. Then sum over u=1..s-1 dp_{s-u} (q_s+u)!.
So
dp_s = - \frac{1}{q_s!} \left( (s+q_s)! + \sum_{u=1}^{s-1} dp_{s-u} (q_s+u)! \right).

Divide by (q_s)!:
dp_s = - \left( \frac{(s+q_s)!}{q_s!} + \sum_{u=1}^{s-1} dp_{s-u} \frac{(q_s+u)!}{q_s!} \right)
= - \left( (q_s+1)_s + \sum_{u=1}^{s-1} dp_{s-u} (q_s+1)_u \right)
where (x)_u = x(x+1)...(x+u-1) rising factorial.

So dp_s depends on previous dp_{s-u} weighted by rising factorial (q_s+1)_u. This is like evaluating a polynomial P_s(z) = ∑_{u=1}^{s-1} dp_{s-u} (z+1)_u at z=q_s, plus constant. If we maintain for each s the polynomial of previous dp? Not.

But note q_s = p_s - s is the balance D_s. The active constraints have q_s≥0. In the original path, q_s can be interpreted as height. The rising factorial (q+1)_u is the number of ways to choose? Maybe there is a combinatorial interpretation leading to a simple product.

Let's compute dp_s for p_s=s+q with constant q. Then recurrence:
dp_s = - [ (s+q)!/q! + ∑_{u=1}^{s-1} dp_{s-u} (q+u)!/q! ].
Let a_s = dp_s * q! / (s+q)!? Maybe.
Define b_s = dp_s / P(s+q, s)? Since P(s+q,s)=(s+q)!/q!. Then:
dp_s = b_s * (s+q)!/q!.
Recurrence:
b_s * (s+q)!/q! = - (s+q)!/q! - ∑_{u=1}^{s-1} b_{s-u} * ((s-u+q)! / q!) * ((q+u)!/q!).
Divide (s+q)!/q!:
b_s = -1 - ∑_{u=1}^{s-1} b_{s-u} * \frac{(s-u+q)! (q+u)!}{q! (s+q)!}.
The ratio = \frac{(s-u+q)! (q+u)!}{q! (s+q)!} = \frac{1}{\binom{s+q}{u}?} Let's compute:
(s+q)! / ((s-u+q)! (q+u)!) =? Not simple but maybe = \binom{s+q}{u} *? Actually \binom{s+q}{u} = (s+q)!/(u!(s+q-u)!) = (s+q)!/(u!(s-u+q)!). So (s-u+q)!/(s+q)! = 1/(u! \binom{s+q}{u}). Then ratio = (q+u)!/(q! u! \binom{s+q}{u}) = \binom{q+u}{u} / \binom{s+q}{u}. So
b_s = -1 - ∑_{u=1}^{s-1} b_{s-u} * \frac{\binom{q+u}{u}}{\binom{s+q}{u}}.
Still not telescoping.

If q=0, ratio = 1 / \binom{s}{u}. Recurrence b_s = -1 - ∑ b_{s-u}/\binom{s}{u}. Not simple.

We need for each s active:

Let B = 700. Process s increasing. Maintain:
- For completed blocks of t, precompute contribution to all future p? We can compute block contribution on the fly using convolution per block? Number of blocks ~ N/B ~ 286. For each block, when it is completed, we could compute its contribution to all future s by iterating over future s and summing over t in block? That would be O(N * block_size) if naive? Actually for each block, for each future s, sum over t in block dp[t] fact[p[s]-t]. If we do this for all blocks, total O((N/B)*N*B)=O(N^2) if naive. But maybe we only need output at future p values, which are increasing. We can compute block contribution incrementally as p increases using the recurrence of factorial:
For a fixed block, contribution C(p) = ∑_{t in block} dp[t] fact[p-t].
As p increases by 1, C(p+1) = ∑ dp[t] (p+1-t) fact[p-t] = (p+1)∑ dp[t] fact[p-t] - ∑ dp[t] t fact[p-t].
So if we maintain two sums for each block: C0(p)=∑ dp[t] fact[p-t] and C1(p)=∑ dp[t] t fact[p-t], then C0(p+1) = (p+1) C0(p) - C1(p). But C1(p) also evolves: C1(p+1)=∑ dp[t] t (p+1-t) fact[p-t] = (p+1)C1(p) - ∑ dp[t] t^2 fact[p-t]. Need higher moments. In general, to update factorial shift, we need moments of all orders because (p+1-t) multiplies by t. Not finite.

Maybe use "online FFT" with block size and NTT per block of size B, total O(N^2/B + N B log B)? Let's design a block algorithm for online convolution with fixed kernel h.

For standard recurrence f[i] = g[i] + ∑_{j<i} f[j] h[i-j], block method:
Process blocks of size B. For each block [L,R), before computing f in block, we have contributions from all previous blocks. We can maintain an array C where C[i] accumulates contributions from completed blocks to future i. When a block is completed, compute its contribution to all future positions by convolving the block's f values with h, and add to C. This convolution length B with h length N costs O(N log N) per block if full, too much. But we only need contributions up to N, total blocks N/B, cost O(N^2/B log N). With B~sqrt N, O(N^1.5). Not good.

(n+1)A_n = (n+1)n! - ∑_{k=1}^{n-1} A_k (n+1)(n-k)!.

The nonzero dp constraints have strictly increasing p. Let their original indices s_i and p_i. The recurrence:
dp_i = -invfact[p_i - s_i] * (fact[p_i] + ∑_{j<i} dp_j fact[p_i - s_j]).
This is similar to a triangular system. If we define a matrix M_{i,j} = fact[p_i - s_j] for j≤i, and vector b_i = -fact[p_i], with diagonal? Actually dp_i * fact[p_i - s_i] + ∑_{j<i} dp_j fact[p_i - s_j] = -fact[p_i]. This is a lower triangular linear system:
∑_{j≤i} dp_j fact[p_i - s_j] = -fact[p_i].
So dp is the solution of M dp = -f, where M_{i,j} = fact[p_i - s_j] for j≤i. This is a "Cauchy" matrix with factorial entries. The answer is N! + ∑ dp_i (N-s_i)!.

This linear system might have a known inverse: The inverse of the matrix with entries fact[p_i - s_j] is related to binomial coefficients and can be computed in O(K) because it is the transition matrix between bases of polynomials? Let's investigate.

Let s_j and p_i be two sequences. The matrix A_{i,j} = fact[p_i - s_j] = (p_i - s_j)!.
There is a known identity: The inverse of the matrix [(x_i - y_j)!] is given by divided differences. Since factorial is not polynomial, but over integers, the matrix with entries 1/(x_i - y_j) (Cauchy) has explicit inverse. For factorial, maybe use the identity (p_i - s_j)! = p_i! / (p_i)_{s_j}? Not.

If we divide rows by fact[p_i] and columns by invfact[s_j]? A_{i,j} = fact[p_i] * invfact[p_i] * fact[p_i - s_j]. Not.

Consider the binomial coefficient C(p_i, s_j) = fact[p_i]/(fact[s_j] fact[p_i-s_j]). Our matrix is fact[p_i-s_j] = fact[p_i] / (fact[s_j] C(p_i,s_j))? Actually C(p_i,s_j)=fact[p_i]/(fact[s_j] fact[p_i-s_j]), so fact[p_i-s_j] = fact[p_i]/(fact[s_j] C(p_i,s_j)). Not simpler.

Maybe the inverse of M can be computed using the "binomial transform" if p_i and s_j are related. In our case p_i and s_i are both increasing and p_i ≥ s_i. We'll have arrays:
q_list = [q for active] (1-index)
p_list = [p_q]
dp = [0]*K
acc = [0]*K (accumulated conv[p_q] from previous constraints)
solve(l,r):
 if r-l==1:
   i=l; q=q_list[i]; p=p_list[i]
   val = fact[p] + acc[i]
   dp[i] = - val * invfact[p-q] % MOD
   return
 mid=(l+r)//2
 solve(l,mid)
 # add contributions from left to right
 build A from dp[l:mid] with index q_list[j]
 min_t = q_list[l]; max_t = q_list[mid-1]
 min_r = min(p_list[mid:r]); max_r = max(p_list[mid:r]) (p_list increasing so min=p_list[mid], max=p_list[r-1])
 min_k = min_r - max_t
 max_k = max_r - min_t
 if min_k <= max_k:
   A length max_t-min_t+1, fill A[q-min_t]=dp
   B length max_k-min_k+1, B[k-min_k]=fact[k]
   conv = convolution(A,B)
   for i in mid..r-1:
      idx = p_list[i] - min_t - min_k
      acc[i] = (acc[i] + conv[idx]) % MOD
 solve(mid,r)

Need ensure idx within conv length. conv length = lenA+lenB-1 = (max_t-min_t+1)+(max_k-min_k+1)-1 = max_t-min_t+max_k-min_k+1. idx max = max_r - min_t - min_k = max_r - min_t - (min_r - max_t) = (max_r-min_r)+(max_t-min_t) ≤ len-1. Good.

But p_list is nondecreasing? p_q = a_{q+1} nondecreasing, and active list preserves order, so p_list nondecreasing. Thus min_r=p_list[mid], max_r=p_list[r-1].

Now, fact array length N+1. B indices min_k..max_k must be within 0..N. min_k = min_r - max_t. Since min_r = p_i for i=mid, and max_t < q_i? For right i, q_i > max_t. Active p_i ≥ q_i, so min_r ≥ q_i > max_t, so min_k ≥1. max_k ≤ N-1 - min_t ≤ N-2. So fact available.

Now, need compute p_q from S. Let's write function:
def compute_p(N,S):
    p = [0]*(N)  # index q-1 maybe, but we'll use 1-index list
    w=0; b=0
    for ch in S:
       if ch=='W':
          w+=1
       else:
          b+=1
          if b>=2:
             # this is B_{b}, q = b-1? Wait p_q = a_{q+1} = #W before B_{q+1}.
             # When processing B with count b, if b>=2, this is B_{b} = B_{q+1} where q=b-1.
             # p_q = w before this B.
             p_q = w
             q = b-1

def ntt(a, invert):
    n=len(a)
    j=0 bit-reversal? Use iterative Cooley with bit-reversal permutation. Standard.
    # bit reversal
    for i in range(1,n):
       bit=n>>1
       while j & bit:
          j ^= bit; bit >>=1
       j ^= bit
       if i<j: swap
    len_=2
    while len_<=n:
       wlen = pow(root, (MOD-1)//len_, MOD)
       if invert: wlen = pow(wlen, MOD-2, MOD)
       for i in range(0,n,len_):
          w=1
          half=len_//2
          for j in range(i,i+half):
             u=a[j]; v=a[j+half]*w%MOD
             a[j]=(u+v)%MOD
             a[j+half]=(u-v)%MOD
             w=w*wlen%MOD
       len_ <<=1
    if invert:
       inv_n=pow(n, MOD-2, MOD)
       for i in range(n): a[i]=a[i]*inv_n%MOD

This is standard but bit reversal O(n log n) okay. For performance, use iterative with precomputed roots? Could optimize but fine.

    if n==0 or m==0: return []

def solve(l,r):
    if r-l==1: compute dp
    mid=(l+r)//2
    solve(l,mid)
    # contribution
    if (mid-l)*(r-mid) <= 4000:
       for i in range(mid,r):
          pi=p_list[i]
          s=0
          for j in range(l,mid):
             s = (s + dp[j]*fact[pi - q_list[j]]) % MOD
          acc[i] = (acc[i]+s)%MOD
    else:
       min_t = q_list[l]; max_t = q_list[mid-1]
       min_r = p_list[mid]; max_r = p_list[r-1]  # p increasing
       min_k = min_r - max_t
       max_k = max_r - min_t
       if min_k <= max_k:
          A = [0]*(max_t-min_t+1)
          for j in range(l,mid):
             A[q_list[j]-min_t] = dp[j]
          B = [0]*(max_k-min_k+1)
          for k in range(min_k, max_k+1):

          C = convolution(A,B)
          for i in range(mid,r):
             idx = p_list[i] - min_t - min_k
             acc[i] = (acc[i] + C[idx]) % MOD
    solve(mid,r)

def ntt(a, invert):
    n=len(a)
    h=(n.bit_length()-1)
    # bit reversal permutation
    j=0
    for i in range(1,n):
       bit=n>>1
       while j & bit:
          j ^= bit
          bit >>=1
       j ^= bit
       if i<j: a[i],a[j]=a[j],a[i]
    length=2
    while length<=n:
       wlen=pow(3,(MOD-1)//length,MOD)
       if invert: wlen=pow(wlen,MOD-2,MOD)
       half=length>>1
       for i in range(0,n,length):
          w=1
          end=i+half
          for j in range(i,end):
             u=a[j]; v=a[j+half]*w%MOD
             x=u+v
             if x>=MOD: x-=MOD
             y=u-v
             if y<0: y+=MOD
             a[j]=x; a[j+half]=y
             w=w*wlen%MOD
       length <<=1
    if invert:
       inv_n=pow(n,MOD-2,MOD)
       for i in range(n): a[i]=a[i]*inv_n%MOD

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353
G = 3

def ntt(a, invert=False):
    n = len(a)
    j = 0
    for i in range(1, n):
        bit = n >> 1
        while j & bit:
            j ^= bit
            bit >>= 1
        j ^= bit
        if i < j:
            a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]

    length = 2
    while length <= n:
        wlen = pow(G, (MOD - 1) // length, MOD)
        if invert:
            wlen = pow(wlen, MOD - 2, MOD)
        half = length >> 1
        for i in range(0, n, length):
            w = 1
            for j in range(i, i + half):
                u = a[j]
                v = a[j + half] * w % MOD
                x = u + v
                if x >= MOD:
                    x -= MOD
                y = u - v
                if y < 0:
                    y += MOD
                a[j] = x
                a[j + half] = y
                w = w * wlen % MOD
        length <<= 1

    if invert:
        inv_n = pow(n, MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(n):
            a[i] = a[i] * inv_n % MOD


def convolution(a, b):
    n = len(a)
    m = len(b)
    if n == 0 or m == 0:
        return []
    if n * m <= 200000:
        res = [0] * (n + m - 1)
        for i, ai in enumerate(a):
            if ai:
                for j, bj in enumerate(b):
                    res[i + j] = (res[i + j] + ai * bj) % MOD
        return res

    size = 1
    while size < n + m - 1:
        size <<= 1
    fa = a[:] + [0] * (size - n)
    fb = b[:] + [0] * (size - m)
    ntt(fa, False)
    ntt(fb, False)
    for i in range(size):
        fa[i] = fa[i] * fb[i] % MOD
    ntt(fa, True)
    return fa[:n + m - 1]


def main():
    input_data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not input_data:
        return
    N = int(input_data[0])
    S = input_data[1].strip()

    if S[0] == 'W' or S[-1] == 'B':
        print(0)
        return

    fact = [1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD
    invfact = [1] * (N + 1)
    invfact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(N, 0, -1):
        invfact[i - 1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD

    # p[q] = number of W before B_{q+1}, for q = 1..N-1
    p_arr = [0] * N
    w = 0
    b = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch == 'W':
            w += 1
        else:
            b += 1
            if b >= 2:
                p_arr[b - 1] = w

    # Active constraints: p_q >= q.
    # If the same p appears again, later dp is always 0, so keep only the first.
    active = []
    last_p = -1
    for q in range(1, N):
        p = p_arr[q]
        if p >= q and p != last_p:
            active.append((q, p))
            last_p = p

    K = len(active)
    if K == 0:
        print(fact[N] % MOD)
        return

    q_list = [x[0] for x in active]
    p_list = [x[1] for x in active]
    dp = [0] * K
    acc = [0] * K

    import sys
    sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

    def solve(l, r):
        if r - l == 1:
            i = l
            q = q_list[i]
            p = p_list[i]
            val = fact[p] + acc[i]
            val %= MOD
            dp[i] = (-val * invfact[p - q]) % MOD
            return

        mid = (l + r) // 2
        solve(l, mid)

        # contributions from [l, mid) to [mid, r)
        if (mid - l) * (r - mid) <= 5000:
            for i in range(mid, r):
                pi = p_list[i]
                s = 0
                for j in range(l, mid):
                    s += dp[j] * fact[pi - q_list[j]]
                acc[i] = (acc[i] + s) % MOD
        else:
            min_t = q_list[l]
            max_t = q_list[mid - 1]
            min_r = p_list[mid]
            max_r = p_list[r - 1]

            min_k = min_r - max_t
            max_k = max_r - min_t

            if min_k <= max_k:
                A = [0] * (max_t - min_t + 1)
                for j in range(l, mid):
                    A[q_list[j] - min_t] = dp[j]

                B = [0] * (max_k - min_k + 1)
                for k in range(min_k, max_k + 1):
                    B[k - min_k] = fact[k]

                C = convolution(A, B)

                for i in range(mid, r):
                    idx = p_list[i] - min_t - min_k
                    acc[i] = (acc[i] + C[idx]) % MOD

        solve(mid, r)

    solve(0, K)

    ans = fact[N]
    for i in range(K):
        q = q_list[i]
        ans = (ans + dp[i] * fact[N - q]) % MOD

    print(ans % MOD)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
